SARMA: Scalable Low-Rank High-Dimensional Autoregressive Moving Averages via Tensor Decomposition
Feiqing Huang, Kexin Li, Yao Zheng

Abstract
This supplementary file contains six sections. Section S1 develops a method for selecting the response and predictor ranks, which is further proved to be consistent. Section S2 presents algorithms for the proposed rank-constrained and SLTR estimators. Section S3 presents simulation studies for verifying the theoretical results. Section S4 provides descriptions of datasets for the empirical examples in the main paper. All technical proofs for the theoretical results in Sections 2 and 3 in main paper are provided in Sections S5 and S6 , respectively.



Keywords
: High-dimensional time series, Identifiability, Reduced-rank regression, Scalability, Tensor decomposition, VAR(
\infty
), VARMA

MSC2020 subject classifications
: Primary 62M10; secondary 62H12, 60G10

1
Introduction

Consider the vector autoregressive (VAR) model of order
1\leq P\leq\infty
for an
N
-dimensional time series
\{\boldsymbol{y}_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}
,

\boldsymbol{y}_{t}=\sum_{j=1}^{P}\boldsymbol{A}_{j}\boldsymbol{y}_{t-j}+\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t},

(1.1)

where
\boldsymbol{A}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
are the AR coefficient matrices, and

\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
is the innovation term at time
t
.
VAR models are fundamental in multivariate time series modeling
(
Lütkepohl,, 2005
;
Tsay,, 2014
)
. Their versatility have led to numerous extensions to accommodate more complex dynamic structures and high dimensions
(
Basu and Matteson,, 2021
;
Wang et al.,, 2022
;
Bai et al.,, 2023
, e.g.,)
.
However, the VAR model is typically employed with a small lag order
P
in practice; or theoretically,
P
is fixed as the sample size and dimension increase. This amounts to assuming that the present observation
\boldsymbol{y}_{t}
is influenced by only a few small lags,
\boldsymbol{y}_{t-j}
. By excluding data from further in the past, this significantly limits the flexibility of the VAR model. In this paper, we address this limitation by developing a dimension reduction approach to accommodate
P=\infty
. Note that it includes the finite-order VAR as a special case, yet eliminates the need for order selection.

Let
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N\times\infty}
be the AR coefficient tensor defined by stacking
\boldsymbol{A}_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq P=\infty
. To address the large dimension
N
, this paper considers a low-Tucker-rank approach for dimension reduction in the first two modes of
\mathscr{A}
. When the cross-sectional dependency among the
N
component series is sparse, it is natural to impose sparsity on AR coefficient matrices
(
Basu and Michailidis,, 2015
;
Zheng,, 2025
, e.g.,)
. However, in many applications, especially in economics and finance, the component series often exhibit co-movements and are believed to be driven by a small number of common factors. In such scenarios, the cross-sectional dependency would be non-sparse. For VAR models with a fixed order
P
,
Wang et al., (2022)
imposes a low-Tucker-rank assumption on
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N\times P}
for simultaneous dimension reduction across all three modes of the coefficient tensor. However, it remains an open question how to extend this approach to
P=\infty
.

To accommodate both large
N
and
P=\infty
, this paper introduces a novel solution, which can be encapsulated by the following decomposition of
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N\times\infty}
:

\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{S}$}\times_{1}\boldsymbol{U}_{1}\times_{2}\boldsymbol{U}_{2}\times_{3}\boldsymbol{L}(\boldsymbol{\omega}),

(1.2)

where

\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{S}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{\pazocal{R}_{1}\times\pazocal{R}_{2}\times\pazocal{R}_{3}}
and
\boldsymbol{U}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times\pazocal{R}_{i}}
for
i=1,2
are unknown parameter tensor and matrices, and
\boldsymbol{L}(\boldsymbol{\omega})\in\mathbb{R}^{\infty\times\pazocal{R}_{3}}
is a predetermined function with an unknown low-dimensional parameter vector
\boldsymbol{\omega}
. Similar to
Wang et al., (2022)
, this implies that
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N\times\infty}
has low Tucker ranks,
(\pazocal{R}_{1},\pazocal{R}_{2},\pazocal{R}_{3})
. However, instead of factoring out an unrestricted matrix
\boldsymbol{U}_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{\infty\times\pazocal{R}_{3}}
at the third mode, we deal with the infinite dimension via further parameterization. As a result, the number of parameters is reduced from infinity to
O(N)
. It is worth noting that our theoretical analysis will differ substantially from
Wang et al., (2022)
due to the possible nonlinearity of the general parameterization for
\boldsymbol{L}
. The conjunction of (
1.1
) and (
1.2
) leads to a VAR process with simultaneous scalability across response, predictor, and temporal dimensions, allowing large
N
and
P=\infty
. Moreover, we discover that the factorization of
\boldsymbol{L}(\boldsymbol{\omega})
is closely related to vector autoregressive moving average (VARMA) models; in other words, VARMA models are essentially low-rank VAR(
\infty
) models.
Therefore, we call the proposed high-dimensional low-Tucker-rank VAR(
\infty
) model the
scalable ARMA (SARMA)
model.

Recently, there has been growing interest in the estimation of large and high-dimensional VARMA models
(
Athanasopoulos and Vahid,, 2008
;
Chan et al.,, 2016
;
Dias and Kapetanios,, 2018
;
Wilms et al.,, 2023
, e.g.,)
. However, VARMA models require complex identification constraints, which often obscure the interpretation of the parameters. On the other hand,
Zheng, (2025)
develops a sparse VAR(
\infty
) model which is motivated by the VARMA model but resolves the identification issue. Interestingly, we can show that the model in
Zheng, (2025)
is a special case of (
1.2
) without factorizations of
\boldsymbol{U}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{U}_{2}
, i.e., there is no low-rank structure for the first two modes. The parametric approach in
Zheng, (2025)
indeed constitutes an easy-to-implement specification for the user-defined function
\boldsymbol{L}(\boldsymbol{\omega})
. Furthermore, we prove the identifiability of the model for the first time in this paper.

Additionally, in the ultra-high-dimensional setup where
N
may grow exponentially with the sample size, we further consider a sparse low-Tucker-rank (SLTR) structure for
\mathscr{A}
by imposing entrywise-sparsity on the loadings of the response and predictor factors. This results in a more substantial dimension reduction and can be interpreted as an automatic selection of important variables into the response and predictor factors. For the proposed SARMA model, we introduce two estimators: (i) the rank-constrained estimator for the case with non-sparse factor loadings, and (ii) the SLTR estimator for the case with sparse factor loadings. For both estimators, we derive nonasymptotic error bounds. A consistent estimator for the Tucker ranks, method for model order selection, and algorithms for implementing the proposed methods are detailed in the supplementary file.

The rest of this paper is organized as follows. Section
2
introduces the proposed model, including a general formulation, representative examples, the high-dimensional SARMA model, and the dynamic factor interpretations of the latter. Section
3
develops estimation methods in both non-sparse and sparse cases, together with theoretical properties. Empirical studies are provided in Section
4
. Section
5
concludes with a brief discussion. Due to the page limit, methods for rank and model order selection, simulation studies, algorithms, and technical details are given in a separate supplementary file.

1.1
Notations

Unless otherwise specified, we denote scalars by lowercase letters
x,y,\dots
, vectors by boldface lowercase letters
\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y},\dots
, and matrices by boldface capital letters
\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y},\dots
.
For any
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, denote
a\vee b=\max\{a,b\}
and
a\wedge b=\min\{a,b\}
.
For any vector
\boldsymbol{x}
, denote its
\ell_{2}
norm by
\|\boldsymbol{x}\|_{2}
.
For any matrix
\boldsymbol{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}}
, let
\sigma_{1}(\boldsymbol{X})\geq\sigma_{2}(\boldsymbol{X})\geq\cdots\geq\sigma_{d_{1}\wedge d_{2}}(\boldsymbol{X})\geq 0
be its singular values in descending order. Let
\boldsymbol{X}^{\prime}
,
\sigma_{\max}(\boldsymbol{X})
(or
\sigma_{\min}(\boldsymbol{X})
),
\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{X})
(or
\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{X})
), and
\rank(\boldsymbol{X})
denote its transpose, largest (or smallest) singular value, largest (or smallest) eigenvalue, and rank, respectively.
Its vectorization
\vect(\boldsymbol{X})
is the long vector obtained by stacking all its columns.
In addition, its operator norm, Frobenius norm, and nuclear norm are
\|\boldsymbol{X}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\sigma_{\max}(\boldsymbol{X})
,
\|\boldsymbol{X}\|_{\mathrm{F}}=\sqrt{\sum_{i,j}\boldsymbol{X}_{ij}^{2}}=\sqrt{\sum_{k=1}^{d_{1}\wedge d_{2}}\sigma_{k}^{2}(\boldsymbol{X})}
, and
\|\boldsymbol{X}\|_{*}=\sum_{k=1}^{d_{1}\wedge d_{2}}\sigma_{k}(\boldsymbol{X})
, respectively. For any two sequences
x_{n}
and
y_{n}
, denote
x_{n}\lesssim y_{n}
(or
x_{n}\gtrsim y_{n}
) if there exists an absolute constant
C>0
such that
x_{n}\leq Cy_{n}
(or
x_{n}\geq Cy_{n}
). Write
x_{n}\asymp y_{n}
if
x_{n}\lesssim y_{n}
and
x_{n}\gtrsim y_{n}
. Let
\mathbb{I}_{\{\cdot\}}
be the indicator function taking value one when the condition is true and zero otherwise. The capital letters
C,C_{\mbox{\boldmath\tiny$\mathscr{G}$}},\dots
and lowercase letters
c,c_{\mbox{\boldmath\tiny$\mathscr{G}$}},\dots
represent generic large and small positive absolute constants, respectively, whose values may vary from place to place.

This paper involves third-order tensors, a.k.a. three-way arrays, which are denoted by calligraphic capital letters. For example, a
d_{1}\times d_{2}\times d_{3}
tensor is
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}=(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}_{i_{1}i_{2}i_{3}})_{1\leq i_{1}\leq d_{1},1\leq i_{2}\leq d_{2},1\leq i_{3}\leq d_{3}}
. It has three modes, with dimension
d_{i}
for mode
i
, for
1\leq i\leq 3
. The Frobenius norm of the tensor is defined as
\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}\|_{{\mathrm{F}}}=\sqrt{\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}_{i_{1}i_{2}i_{3}}^{2}}
. The mode-3 product of
\mathscr{X}
and a
K\times d_{3}
matrix
\boldsymbol{Y}
is the
d_{1}\times d_{2}\times K
tensor given by
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}\times_{3}\boldsymbol{Y}=(\sum_{i_{3}=1}^{d_{3}}\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}_{i_{1}i_{2}i_{3}}\boldsymbol{Y}_{ki_{3}})_{1\leq i_{1}\leq d_{1},1\leq i_{2}\leq d_{2},1\leq k\leq K}
. Similarly, the mode-
i
multiplication
\times_{i}
between
\mathscr{X}
and a
K\times d_{i}
matrix can be defined for
i=1,2
.
The matricization along mode
i
of
\mathscr{X}
results in a matrix where the mode
i
becomes the rows of the matrix, and the other modes are collapsed into the columns. The mode-
i
matricization is denoted by
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}_{(i)}
, and it can be shown that
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}_{(1)}=(\boldsymbol{X}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{X}_{d_{3}})
,

\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}_{(2)}=(\boldsymbol{X}_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\boldsymbol{X}_{d_{3}}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}\times d_{1}d_{3}}
, and
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}_{(3)}=(\text{vec}(\boldsymbol{X}_{1}),\dots,\text{vec}(\boldsymbol{X}_{d_{3}}))^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{3}\times d_{1}d_{2}}
.
The Tucker rank of
\mathscr{X}
at mode
i
is the rank of
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}_{(i)}
, i.e.,
\pazocal{R}_{i}=\textrm{rank}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{X}$}_{(i)})
for
1\leq i\leq 3

(
Tucker,, 1966
;
De Lathauwer et al.,, 2000
)
. Unlike row and column ranks of a matrix,
\pazocal{R}_{1},\pazocal{R}_{2}
and
\pazocal{R}_{3}
in general are not identical.

2
Proposed model

2.1
General formulation

We propose a general VAR(
\infty
) framework as follows:

\boldsymbol{y}_{t}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{A}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\omega},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$})\boldsymbol{y}_{t-j}+\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t},\quad\text{with}\quad\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}(\boldsymbol{\omega},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}\times_{3}\boldsymbol{L}(\boldsymbol{\omega}),

(2.1)

where
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}(\boldsymbol{\omega},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$})\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N\times\infty}
is the AR coefficient tensor formed by stacking
\boldsymbol{A}_{j}=\boldsymbol{A}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\omega},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$})
for
j\geq 1
. In addition,
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N\times d}
is the tensor formed by stacking
\boldsymbol{G}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
for
1\leq k\leq d
, and
\boldsymbol{L}(\boldsymbol{\omega})\in\mathbb{R}^{\infty\times d}
is a user-defined function dependent on
\boldsymbol{\omega}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}
, with a finite-dimensional parameter space
\boldsymbol{\Omega}
. Here
the unknown parameters are
\mathscr{G}
and
\boldsymbol{\omega}
.

Note that
\mathscr{A}
is factorized into
\mathscr{G}
and
\boldsymbol{L}(\boldsymbol{\omega})
along the third mode. It implies that the Tucker rank of
\mathscr{A}
at the third mode is
\pazocal{R}_{3}=d
. This tensor factorization is equivalent to

\boldsymbol{A}_{j}=\boldsymbol{A}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\omega},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$})=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\ell_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\omega})\boldsymbol{G}_{k},\quad\text{for}\quad j\geq 1.

(2.2)

If
\mathscr{G}
is Tucker-low-rank at the first two modes, we further have the factorization in (
1.2
), where
\pazocal{R}_{3}=d
. The low-Tucker-rank VAR(
\infty
) model to be developed in this paper, the sparse VAR(
\infty
) model in
Zheng, (2025)
, and even the VARMA model, can all be regarded as special cases of (
2.1
); we discuss this further in the next subsection.

We establish the stationarity condition for model (
2.1
) under the following assumption on
\boldsymbol{L}(\boldsymbol{\omega})=(\ell_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\omega}))_{j\geq 1,1\leq k\leq d}
.

Assumption 1
.

There exists
0<\rho<1
and
C>0
such that
\max_{1\leq k\leq K}|\ell_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\omega})|\leq C\rho^{j}
for
j\geq 1
.

Theorem 1

(Weak stationarity)
.

Suppose that Assumption
1
holds and
\{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\}
is an
i.i.d.
sequence with
E(\|\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}\|_{2})<\infty
. If
\sum_{k=1}^{d}\|\boldsymbol{G}_{k}\|_{{\mathrm{op}}}<1/\rho-1
,
then there exists a unique weakly stationary solution to model (
2.1
), and it has the form of
\boldsymbol{y}_{t}=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{j}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t-j}
, where

\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{j_{1}+\cdots+j_{k}=j}\boldsymbol{A}_{j_{1}}\cdots\boldsymbol{A}_{j_{k}}
, and

\boldsymbol{A}_{j}
are given by (
2.2
).

2.2
Motivation and representative examples of model (
2.1
)

The formulation in (
2.1
) is intrinsically motivated by an interesting discovery: VARMA models are essentially Tucker-low-rank VAR(
\infty
) processes, where the low-rankness occurs at the third mode of the AR coefficient tensor
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N\times\infty}
and can be parameterized in the form of
\boldsymbol{L}(\boldsymbol{\omega})
.

To show this, consider an invertible VARMA(
p,q
) model in the form of
\boldsymbol{y}_{t}=\sum_{i=1}^{p}\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{i}\boldsymbol{y}_{t-i}+\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}-\sum_{j=1}^{q}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t-j}
, where
\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{i},\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
are the coefficient matrices. Denote the MA companion matrix
(
Lütkepohl,, 2005
)
by

\underline{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}=\left(\begin{matrix}\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{1}&\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{2}&\cdots&\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{q-1}&\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{q}\\
\boldsymbol{I}&\boldsymbol{0}&\cdots&\boldsymbol{0}&\boldsymbol{0}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\boldsymbol{I}&\cdots&\boldsymbol{0}&\boldsymbol{0}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\boldsymbol{0}&\cdots&\boldsymbol{I}&\boldsymbol{0}\end{matrix}\right).

Let
\underline{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
have
R+2S
nonzero eigenvalues, where
R=\sum_{k=1}^{r}n_{j}
and
S=\sum_{k=1}^{s}m_{k}
account for real and complex eigenvalues, respectively. Specifically, let the distinct nonzero real eigenvalues be
\lambda_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq r
, with algebraic multiplicity
n_{j}
. Let the distinct conjugate pairs of complex eigenvalues be
(\gamma_{k}e^{i\theta_{k}},\gamma_{k}e^{-i\theta_{k}})
for
1\leq k\leq s
, with algebraic multiplicity
m_{k}
, where
\gamma_{k}\in(0,1)
and
\theta_{k}\in(-\pi/2,\pi/2)
.

Proposition 1
.

If the geometric multiplicities of all nonzero eigenvalues of
\underline{\boldsymbol{\Theta}}
are one, then for all
j\geq 1
, any invertible VARMA(
p,q
) model can be written in the form of (
1.1
) with
P=\infty
and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\boldsymbol{A}_{j}&=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\mathbb{I}_{\{j=k\}}\boldsymbol{G}_{k}+\sum_{k=1}^{r}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{k}}\mathbb{I}_{\{j\geq p+(i-1)\vee 1\}}\lambda_{k}^{j-p-i+1}\binom{j-p}{i-1}\boldsymbol{G}_{k,i}^{I}\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\sum_{k=1}^{s}\sum_{i=1}^{m_{k}}\mathbb{I}_{\{j\geq p+(i-1)\vee 1\}}\gamma_{k}^{j-p-i+1}\binom{j-p}{i-1}\\
&\hskip 62.59605pt\cdot\left[\cos\{(j-p-i+1)\theta_{k}\}\boldsymbol{G}_{k,i}^{II,1}+\sin\{(j-p-i+1)\theta_{k}\}\boldsymbol{G}_{k,i}^{II,2}\right],\end{split}

(2.3)

where

\boldsymbol{G}_{k,i}^{I},\boldsymbol{G}_{k,i}^{II,1},\boldsymbol{G}_{k,i}^{II,2}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
are all determined jointly by
\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{i}
’s and
\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{j}
’s, and the corresponding sum will be suppressed if
p,r
or
s
is zero. Moreover,
for any fixed
k
and
i
,
\boldsymbol{G}_{k,i}^{II,h}
for
h=1,2
have the same row and column spaces, and

\rank(\boldsymbol{G}_{j,l}^{I})\leq n_{k}
and
\rank(\boldsymbol{G}_{k,i}^{II,h})\leq 2m_{k}

for all
1\leq j\leq r
,
1\leq k\leq s
,
1\leq l\leq n_{k}
,
1\leq i\leq m_{k}
, and
h=1,2
.

While (
2.3
) looks complicated, it can be simply written as (
2.2
) with
d=p+R+2S
. By tensor algebra, we can rewrite it in a surprisingly succinct form, i.e., the second equation in (
2.1
).
This reveals that the VARMA model in general can be understood as a low-rank dimension reduction approach for VAR(
\infty
) processes.

A special case of (
2.3
) is considered in
Zheng, (2025)
, which corresponds to
n_{1}=\cdots=n_{r}=m_{1}=\cdots=m_{s}=1
, i.e.,
R=r
and
S=s
. In this case,
d=p+r+2s=\pazocal{R}_{3}
, and

\ell_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\omega})=\begin{cases}\mathbb{I}_{\{j=k\}}&\text{if }1\leq k\leq p,\\
\mathbb{I}_{\{j\geq p+1\}}\lambda_{m_{k}}^{j-p}&\text{if }p+1\leq k\leq p+r\\
\mathbb{I}_{\{j\geq p+1\}}\gamma_{n_{k}}^{j-p}[\cos(j-p)\theta_{n_{k}}+\sin(j-p)\theta_{n_{k}}],&\text{if }p+r+1\leq k\leq d,\end{cases}

(2.4)

where
m_{k}=k-p
,
n_{k}=\lceil\frac{k-p-r}{2}\rceil
, and
\boldsymbol{\omega}=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{r},\gamma_{1},\theta_{1},\dots,\gamma_{s},\theta_{s})^{\prime}
.

Remark 1

(Infinite distributed lags)
.

Based on (
2.3
),
\lim_{j\rightarrow\infty}\ell_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\omega})=0
for each
k
, which ensures that the coefficient matrix
\boldsymbol{A}_{j}
diminishes to zero as the lag index
j\rightarrow\infty
. This feature is reminiscent of the infinite distributed lag model
(
Judge et al.,, 1991
)
, which captures the gradually diminishing impact of past values of the variable
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}
on the response
y_{t}\in\mathbb{R}
. For example, in the case with geometric lags, the model is

y_{t}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\beta_{j}x_{t-j}+e_{t}
, where
\beta_{j}=\lambda^{j}\alpha.

Here
\lambda\in(-1,1)
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
are unknown parameters, which are analogous to
\lambda_{k}
and
\boldsymbol{G}_{k}
, respectively. Obviously,
\alpha
is extended to a matrix as the response and predictor in VAR(
\infty
) models are both
N
-dimensional. However, (
2.3
) also extends geometric lags by allowing for various decay patterns.

Other types of decay patterns can be found in the literature on infinite distributed lags, such as the Pascal lags, discounted polynomial lags, Gamma lags and exponential lags
(
Judge et al.,, 1991
)
. Thus, for
\boldsymbol{A}_{j}=\sum_{k}\ell_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\omega})\boldsymbol{G}_{k}
, a modeler may consider alternative functional forms of
\ell_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\omega})
based on their prior knowledge or preferred interpretation, rather than relying solely on (
2.3
).

In the sequel, for succinctness, we will restrict our discussion to the special case in (
2.4
).
However, all theoretical results can be readily extended to general functions
\boldsymbol{L}
under regularity conditions on the decay rate of
\ell_{j,k}(\cdot)
as
j\rightarrow\infty
and their smoothness.

2.3
Identifiability

In what follows, we will focus on the special case in (
2.4
). That is, given orders
(p,r,s)
, the model is written as

\boldsymbol{y}_{t}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{A}_{j}\boldsymbol{y}_{t-j}+\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t},\quad\text{with}\quad\boldsymbol{A}_{j}=\boldsymbol{A}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\omega},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$})=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\ell_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\omega})\boldsymbol{G}_{k},

(2.5)

where
d=p+r+2s
,
\boldsymbol{G}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
for
1\leq k\leq d
, and the parameter space of
\boldsymbol{\omega}
is

\boldsymbol{\Omega}=\{\boldsymbol{\omega}\in\mathbb{R}^{r+2s}\mid|\lambda_{k}|,\gamma_{h}\in(0,1),\theta_{h}\in(0,\pi)\text{ for }1\leq k\leq r\text{ and }1\leq h\leq s\}.

(2.6)

Note that under the specification in (
2.4
),
\ell_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\omega})
is the
(j,k)
-th entry of the matrix

\boldsymbol{L}(\boldsymbol{\omega})=\left(\begin{matrix}\boldsymbol{I}_{p}&\boldsymbol{0}&\cdots&\boldsymbol{0}&\boldsymbol{0}&\cdots&\boldsymbol{0}\\
\boldsymbol{0}&\boldsymbol{\ell}^{I}(\lambda_{1})&\cdots&\boldsymbol{\ell}^{I}(\lambda_{r})&\boldsymbol{\ell}^{II}(\gamma_{1},\theta_{1})&\cdots&\boldsymbol{\ell}^{II}(\gamma_{s},\theta_{s})\end{matrix}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{\infty\times d},

(2.7)

where

\boldsymbol{\ell}^{I}(\lambda)=(\lambda,\lambda^{2},\lambda^{3},\dots)^{\prime}\quad\text{and}\quad\boldsymbol{\ell}^{II}(\gamma,\theta)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\gamma\cos(\theta)&\gamma^{2}\cos(2\theta)&\gamma^{3}\cos(3\theta)&\cdots\\
\gamma\sin(\theta)&\gamma^{2}\sin(2\theta)&\gamma^{3}\sin(3\theta)&\cdots\\
\end{array}\right)^{\prime},

for any
\lambda
and
(\gamma,\theta)
.
Given the model orders
(p,r,s)
, the following theorem implies that the parameters
\boldsymbol{\omega}
and
\boldsymbol{G}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{G}_{d}
for this model are identifiable.

Theorem 2

(Identifiability)
.

Suppose that
\boldsymbol{G}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{G}_{d}\neq\boldsymbol{0}
and
\boldsymbol{\omega}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}
. If
\lambda_{1}<\cdots<\lambda_{r}
, and the pairs
(\gamma_{m},\theta_{m})
’s are distinct and sorted in ascending order of
\gamma_{m}
’s and
\theta_{m}
’s, then there is a one-to-one correspondence between matrices
\{\boldsymbol{A}_{1},\boldsymbol{A}_{2},\dots\}
and
\{\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{G}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{G}_{d}\}
, where
\boldsymbol{A}_{j}
’s are defined as in (
2.5
).

Since any VAR(
\infty
) process is uniquely defined by its AR coefficient matrices
\{\boldsymbol{A}_{1},\boldsymbol{A}_{2},\dots\}
, Theorem
2
establishes the identifiability of
\boldsymbol{\omega}
and
\boldsymbol{G}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{G}_{d}
up to a permutation. Thus, unlike the VARMA model, no additional parameter constraint is needed for the identification of the parameters
\{\boldsymbol{\omega},\boldsymbol{G}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{G}_{d}\}
. Moreover, with
\boldsymbol{A}_{j}
’s parameterized as linear combinations of matrices, the computation for this model avoids any high-order matrix polynomials, which substantially reduces the computational cost compared to the VARMA model.

2.4
The high-dimensional SARMA model

The general formulation in (
2.1
) addresses the third mode of
\mathscr{A}
, i.e., the temporal mode, by imposing
\pazocal{R}_{3}=\rank(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(3)})=d
.
When
N
is large, we need to further conduct the dimension reduction for the first two modes of
\mathscr{A}
, i.e., the
response
and
predictor
modes. Specifically, we impose the low-Tucker-rank assumption on
\mathscr{A}
for its first two modes as follows:

\pazocal{R}_{i}=\rank(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(i)})\ll N,\quad i=1,2.

Note that
\rank(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(i)})=\rank(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}_{(i)})
for
i=1,2
, as the factorizations along different modes of the tensor do not interfere with each other. Thus, this is also equivalent to assuming that
\mathscr{G}
has low Tucker ranks at its first two modes:

\pazocal{R}_{i}=\rank(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}_{(i)})\ll N,\quad i=1,2.

(2.8)

Then, under this assumption, there exist a small tensor
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{S}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{\pazocal{R}_{1}\times\pazocal{R}_{2}\times d}
and full-rank matrices
\boldsymbol{U}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times\pazocal{R}_{i}}
for
i=1,2
such that
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{S}$}\times_{1}\boldsymbol{U}_{1}\times_{2}\boldsymbol{U}_{2}
. Thus,

\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}=\underbrace{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{S}$}\times_{1}\boldsymbol{U}_{1}\times_{2}\boldsymbol{U}_{2}}_{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}}\times_{3}\boldsymbol{L}(\boldsymbol{\omega}):=[\![\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{S}$};\boldsymbol{U}_{1},\boldsymbol{U}_{2},\boldsymbol{L}(\boldsymbol{\omega})]\!],

(2.9)

In tensor algebra, (
2.9
) is called the Tucker decomposition of the tensor
\mathscr{A}
, with
\mathscr{S}
termed the core tensor, and
\boldsymbol{U}_{1},\boldsymbol{U}_{2}
and
\boldsymbol{L}(\boldsymbol{\omega})
termed the factor matrices.
Note that the factorization of
\mathscr{G}
is written mainly to facilitate the understanding of low-Tucker-rank assumption; see Section
2.5
. The unknown parameters to be estimated are still
\boldsymbol{\omega}
and
\boldsymbol{G}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{G}_{d}
(i.e., the tensor
\mathscr{G}
).

Similar to the low-rankness of matrices, the low-Tucker-rank assumption enables a reduction in the number of parameters for the coefficient tensor: it reduces the effective dimension of
\mathscr{G}
from
N^{2}d
to
O(N(\pazocal{R}_{1}+\pazocal{R}_{2})+\pazocal{R}_{1}\pazocal{R}_{2}d)
. From the viewpoint of VAR(
\infty
) modelling, (
2.9
) reveals that a simultaneous dimension reduction is conducted across the response, predictor, and temporal modes of the AR coefficient tensor
\mathscr{A}
.
To emphasize the resulting scalability across all three directions, we name model (
2.5
) with the low-Tucker-rank assumption in (
2.8
) for
\mathscr{G}
the Scalable ARMA (SARMA) model.

2.5
Dynamic factor interpretation

We discuss the interpretation of the low-Tucker-rank assumption in (
2.8
) for the SARMA model and show that it implies low-dimensional dynamic factor structures underlying both the response and lagged predictor series.

As the model is parameterized by
\boldsymbol{\omega}
and
\mathscr{G}
, it is not necessary to construct estimators for the components
\mathscr{S}
,
\boldsymbol{U}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{U}_{2}
in the factorization of
\mathscr{G}
. Nonetheless, (
2.9
) facilitates our understanding of the low-Tucker-rank assumption on
\mathscr{G}
for the VAR(
\infty
) model.
It reveals that while
\boldsymbol{L}(\boldsymbol{\omega})
extracts essential patterns from the temporal mode of the coefficient tensor
\mathscr{A}
, the matrices
\boldsymbol{U}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{U}_{2}
summarize information along the cross-sectional dimension of the response and lagged predictors, respectively. Note that

\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{S}$}\times_{1}\boldsymbol{U}_{1}\times_{2}\boldsymbol{U}_{2}=(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{S}$}\times_{1}\boldsymbol{O}_{1}\times_{2}\boldsymbol{O}_{2})\times_{1}(\boldsymbol{U}_{1}\boldsymbol{O}_{1}^{-1})\times_{2}(\boldsymbol{U}_{2}\boldsymbol{O}_{2}^{-1})

(2.10)

for any invertible matrices
\boldsymbol{O}_{i}
with
i=1,2
, indicating the rotational and scale indeterminacies of the components. Without loss of generality, the normalization constraint

\boldsymbol{U}_{i}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{U}_{i}=\boldsymbol{I}_{\pazocal{R}_{i}}
for
i=1,2
can be imposed to facilitate interpretations.

Moreover, the SARMA model can be interpreted from the factor modelling perspective, with
\boldsymbol{U}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{U}_{2}
representing loading matrices for the response factors and lagged predictor factors, respectively. To see this, first consider the simple example with Tucker ranks
\pazocal{R}_{1}=\pazocal{R}_{2}=1
. In this case,
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{S}$}=(s_{1},\dots,s_{d})^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:=\boldsymbol{s}
and
\boldsymbol{U}_{i}:=\boldsymbol{u}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
for
i=1,2
all reduce to vectors, hence denoted by bold lowercase letters. Then, (
2.9
) implies
\boldsymbol{G}_{k}=s_{k}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\boldsymbol{u}_{2}^{\prime}
for
1\leq k\leq d
, which are rank-one matrices. As a result,
\boldsymbol{A}_{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\ell_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\omega})s_{k}\boldsymbol{u}_{1}\boldsymbol{u}_{2}^{\prime}
for
j\geq 1
. Note that
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{2}
capture patterns from the rows and columns of
\boldsymbol{A}_{j}
’s, respectively. Consequently, with the normalization
\boldsymbol{u}_{i}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{u}_{i}=1
for
i=1,2
, a single-factor model is implied:

\underbrace{\boldsymbol{u}_{1}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{y}_{t}}_{\text{single response factor}}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{d}\ell_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\omega})s_{k}\underbrace{\boldsymbol{u}_{2}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{y}_{t-j}}_{\text{single predictor factor}}+\boldsymbol{e}_{t},

where
\boldsymbol{e}_{t}=\boldsymbol{u}_{1}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}
. For instance, suppose that
\boldsymbol{y}_{t}
contains realized volatilities of
N
stocks in a market. Then
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{y}_{t}
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{2}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{y}_{t-j}
can be viewed as latent
response
and
lagged predictor factors
, respectively, which can also be regarded as two different market volatility indices. The predictor factor loading
\boldsymbol{u}_{2}
encapsulates how the the
past
signals from various stocks are absorbed into the market, while the response factor loading
\boldsymbol{u}_{1}
summarizes the overall response of the
present
market to these signals; see also Section
4
for an empirical example.

For general Tucker ranks
\pazocal{R}_{1}
and
\pazocal{R}_{2}
, analogously we have

\boldsymbol{U}_{1}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{y}_{t}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{d}\ell_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\omega})\boldsymbol{S}_{k}\boldsymbol{U}_{2}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{y}_{t-j}+\boldsymbol{e}_{t}.

(2.11)

Here,
\boldsymbol{U}_{1}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{y}_{t}
represents
\pazocal{R}_{1}
response factors, while

\boldsymbol{U}_{2}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{y}_{t-j}
represents
\pazocal{R}_{2}
lagged predictor factors. with the loading matrices being
\boldsymbol{U}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times\pazocal{R}_{i}}
for
i=1
and 2, respectively. Thus, by imposing the low-Tucker-rank assumption on
\mathscr{G}
in (
2.8
), simultaneous dimension reduction is achieved by extracting factors across both the response and lagged predictors. For convenience, we call
\pazocal{R}_{1}
and
\pazocal{R}_{2}
the
response
and
predictor ranks
, respectively.

In addition, when
N
is extremely large, we may further assume that
\boldsymbol{U}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{U}_{2}
are sparse matrices for more efficient dimension reduction. This implies that each factor contains only a small subset of variables. Take
\boldsymbol{U}_{1}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{y}_{t}
as an example. For
1\leq i\leq N
and
1\leq k\leq\pazocal{R}_{1}
, if the
(i,k)
th entry of
\boldsymbol{U}_{1}
is nonzero, then it implies that the
i
th variable in
\boldsymbol{y}_{t}
is selected into the
k
th response factor. This sparsity assumption, which is embedded in the Tucker decomposition, will make the estimation of the SARMA model more challenging; see Section
3.2
for details.

3
High-dimensional estimation

3.1
Rank-constrained estimator

We first introduce a rank-constrained approach to estimate the parameter vector
\boldsymbol{\omega}
and the low-Tucker-rank parameter tensor
\mathscr{G}
. As will be shown in Section
3.3
, this estimator is consistent under
N=o(T)
, where
T
is the sample size; another estimation method applicable to the ultra-high-dimensional case which allows
\log(N)/T\rightarrow 0
is introduced in Section
3.2
.

Let
\boldsymbol{x}_{t}=(\boldsymbol{y}_{t-1}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{y}_{t-2}^{\prime},\dots)^{\prime}
. Then the squared error loss function is
\mathbb{L}_{T}(\boldsymbol{\omega},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$})=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{y}_{t}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(1)}\boldsymbol{x}_{t}\|_{2}^{2}
, where
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(1)}=(\boldsymbol{A}_{1},\boldsymbol{A}_{2},\dots)
with
\boldsymbol{A}_{j}=\boldsymbol{A}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\omega},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$})=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\ell_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\omega})\boldsymbol{G}_{k}
for
j\geq 1
. Since the loss depends on observations in the infinite past, initial values for
\{\boldsymbol{y}_{t},t\leq 0\}
are needed in practice. We set them to zero for simplicity, that is, let
\boldsymbol{\widetilde{x}}_{t}=(\boldsymbol{y}_{t-1}^{\prime},\dots,\boldsymbol{y}_{1}^{\prime},0,0,\dots)^{\prime}
be the initialized version of
\boldsymbol{x}_{t}
, and define the feasible squared loss function:

\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}_{T}(\boldsymbol{\omega},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$})=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{y}_{t}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}_{(1)}\boldsymbol{\widetilde{x}}_{t}\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\Big\|\boldsymbol{y}_{t}-\sum_{j=1}^{t-1}\boldsymbol{A}_{j}(\boldsymbol{\omega},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$})\boldsymbol{y}_{t-j}\Big\|_{2}^{2}.

(3.1)

The initialization effect will be accounted for in our theoretical analysis.

Suppose that the response and predictor ranks
(\pazocal{R}_{1},\pazocal{R}_{2})
are known; see Section
S1
for a data-driven selection procedure.
When
N
is moderately large compared to
T
, we propose the rank-constrained estimator as follows:

(\boldsymbol{\widehat{\omega}},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{G}}$})=\argmin_{\boldsymbol{\omega}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega},\mbox{\boldmath\scriptsize$\mathscr{G}$}\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(\pazocal{R}_{1},\pazocal{R}_{2})}\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}_{T}(\boldsymbol{\omega},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}),

(3.2)

where
\boldsymbol{\Omega}
is defined in (
2.6
), and the parameter space of
\mathscr{G}
is

\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(\pazocal{R}_{1},\pazocal{R}_{2})=\{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N\times d}\mid\rank(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}_{(1)})\leq\pazocal{R}_{1},\rank(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}_{(2)})\leq\pazocal{R}_{2}\}.

Then based on the results from (
3.2
), we can obtain
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{G}}$}\times_{3}\boldsymbol{L}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{\omega}})
; i.e., the corresponding AR coefficient matrices are estimated by
\boldsymbol{\widehat{A}}_{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\ell_{j,k}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{\omega}})\boldsymbol{\widehat{G}}_{k}
for
j\geq 1
.

Remark 2
.

Note that (
3.2
) does not require estimation of
\mathscr{S}
,
\boldsymbol{U}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{U}_{2}
, i.e., the components in the Tucker decomposition of
\mathscr{G}
. Thus, the rotational and scale indeterminacies in (
2.10
) are not an issue. However, to interpret the underlying dynamic factor structure presented in (
2.11
), it is beneficial to conduct the Tucker decomposition of
\mathscr{\widehat{G}}
to obtain the corresponding estimated loading matrices
\boldsymbol{\widehat{U}}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{\widehat{U}}_{2}
after the rank-constrained estimation in (
3.2
). A common approach to ensure the uniqueness of the Tucker decomposition is to employ the higher-order singular value decomposition (HOSVD), which is the special Tucker decomposition as follows
(
De Lathauwer et al.,, 2000
)
. Specifically, to get the HOSVD,
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{G}}$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{S}}$}\times_{1}\boldsymbol{\widehat{U}}_{1}\times_{2}\boldsymbol{\widehat{U}}_{2}
, the matrix
\boldsymbol{\widehat{U}}_{i}
is defined as the top
\pazocal{R}_{i}
left singular vectors of
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{G}}$}_{(i)}
with the first element in each column of
\boldsymbol{\widehat{U}}_{i}
being positive, for
i=1,2
. This rules out both rotational and sign indeterminacies. In addition, by the orthonormality of
\boldsymbol{\widehat{U}}_{i}
’s, we can compute
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{S}}$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{G}}$}\times_{1}\boldsymbol{\widehat{U}}_{1}^{\prime}\times_{2}\boldsymbol{\widehat{U}}_{2}^{\prime}
. Thus, the factor representation in (
2.11
) for the fitted model can be obtained.
This will allow us to clearly interpret the dynamic factor structure based on the uniquely defined loading matrices
\boldsymbol{\widehat{U}}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{\widehat{U}}_{2}
.

3.2
Sparse low-Tucker-rank estimator

When
N
is very large relative to the sample size
T
, the rank-constrained estimator can be inefficient, and a more substantial dimension reduction is needed. Motivated by the dynamic factor structure in (
2.11
), we additionally assume that the loadings
\boldsymbol{U}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{U}_{2}
are sparse, and develop a high-dimensional estimator that simultaneously enforces the low-Tucker-rank and sparse structures. This not only improves the estimation efficiency but enhances the interpretability as it automatically selects only important variables into the factors.

However, unlike the rank-constrained estimator in (
3.2
), explicit factorization of
\mathscr{G}
must be incorporated into the sparse estimation. Moreover, to ensure the identifiability of the sparsity patterns, we assume that
\boldsymbol{U}_{i}
is the orthonormal matrix consisting of the top
\pazocal{R}_{i}
left singular vectors of
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}_{(i)}
, for
i=1,2
. This implies that
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{S}$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}\times_{1}\boldsymbol{U}_{1}^{\prime}\times_{2}\boldsymbol{U}_{2}^{\prime}
. Note that since
\boldsymbol{U}_{i}
is orthonormal, it can be shown that
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{S}$}_{(i)}
is row-orthogonal, for
i=1,2
.

We consider the following
\ell_{1}
-regularized sparse low-Tucker-rank (SLTR) estimator:

(\boldsymbol{\widetilde{\omega}},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widetilde{S}}$},\boldsymbol{\widetilde{U}}_{1},\boldsymbol{\widetilde{U}}_{2})=\argmin_{\boldsymbol{\omega}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega},\,\mbox{\boldmath\scriptsize$\mathscr{S}$}\in\text{RO}(\pazocal{R}_{1},\pazocal{R}_{2}),\,\boldsymbol{U}_{i}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{U}_{i}=\boldsymbol{I}_{\pazocal{R}_{i}},\,i=1,2}\left\{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}_{T}(\boldsymbol{\omega},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{S}$}\times_{1}\boldsymbol{U}_{1}\times_{2}\boldsymbol{U}_{2})+\lambda\sum_{i=1}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{U}_{i}\|_{1}\right\},

(3.3)

where

\text{RO}(\pazocal{R}_{1},\pazocal{R}_{2})=\{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{S}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{\pazocal{R}_{1}\times\pazocal{R}_{2}\times d}:\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{S}$}_{(i)}\text{ is row-orthogonal},\penalty\ i=1,2\}.

Then it is straightforward to estimate
\mathscr{G}
and
\mathscr{A}
by
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widetilde{G}}$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widetilde{S}}$}\times_{1}\boldsymbol{\widetilde{U}}_{1}\times_{2}\boldsymbol{\widetilde{U}}_{2}
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widetilde{A}}$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widetilde{G}}$}\times_{3}\boldsymbol{L}(\boldsymbol{\widetilde{\omega}})
, respectively; i.e., the estimated coefficient matrices
\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{\widetilde{G}}_{d}
and
\boldsymbol{\widetilde{A}}_{j}
for
j\geq 1
can be obtained.

3.3
Nonasymptotic error bounds

This section provides nonasymptotic error bounds for the proposed rank-constrained and SLTR estimators, in the non-sparse and sparse cases, respectively. We assume that the observed time series
\{\boldsymbol{y}_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}
is generated from a stationary SARMA model with response and predictor ranks
(\pazocal{R}_{1},\pazocal{R}_{2})
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{*}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}
and
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}^{*}\in\boldsymbol{\Gamma}(\pazocal{R}_{1},\pazocal{R}_{2})
denote the true values of
\boldsymbol{\omega}
and
\mathscr{G}
, respectively.
Similarly,
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}
,
\lambda_{k}^{*}
’s,
\gamma_{k}^{*}
’s,
\theta_{k}^{*}
’s, etc., denote the true values of the corresponding parameters.
To prove the consistency of the rank-constrained estimator, we make the following assumptions.

Assumption 2

(Sub-Gaussian error)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t}
, where
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t}
is a sequence of i.i.d. random vectors with zero mean and
\var(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t})=\boldsymbol{I}_{N}
, and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon}
is a positive definite covariance matrix.
In addition, the coordinates
(\boldsymbol{\xi}_{it})_{1\leq i\leq N}
within
\boldsymbol{\xi}_{t}
are mutually independent and
\sigma^{2}
-sub-Gaussian.

Assumption 3

(Parameters)
.

(i) There exists an absolute constant
0<\bar{\rho}<1
such that for all
\boldsymbol{\omega}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}
,
|\lambda_{1}|,\ldots,|\lambda_{r}|,\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{s}\in\Lambda
, where
\Lambda
is a compact subset of
(0,\bar{\rho})
;
(ii) all
\lambda_{k}^{*}
’s are bounded away from each other, and all pairs
(\gamma_{m}^{*},\theta_{m}^{*})
’s are bounded away from each other, for
1\leq k\leq r
and
1\leq m\leq s
;
and (iii)
\max_{1\leq k\leq d}\|\boldsymbol{G}_{k}^{*}\|_{{\mathrm{op}}}\leq C_{\mbox{\boldmath\tiny$\mathscr{G}$}}
for some absolute constant
C_{\mbox{\boldmath\tiny$\mathscr{G}$}}>0
, and
\|\boldsymbol{G}_{k}^{*}\|_{{\mathrm{F}}}\asymp\alpha
for
p+1\leq k\leq d
, where
\alpha=\alpha(N)>0
may depend on the dimension
N
.

Assumption
2
is weaker than the commonly imposed Gaussian assumption in the literature on high-dimensional time series; see, e.g.,
Basu and Michailidis, (2015)
and
Wilms et al., (2023)
. Assumption
3
(i) requires
|\lambda_{k}|
’s and
\gamma_{m}
’s to be bounded away from one. Assumption
3
(ii) ensures that different elements of
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{*}
can be distinguished in the estimation. While Assumption
3
(iii) requires that
\|\boldsymbol{G}_{k}^{*}\|_{{\mathrm{F}}}
for
p+1\leq k\leq d
have the same order of magnitude
\alpha
, it is allowed to vary with
N
. This condition can be readily relaxed through a slightly more involved proof. In this case, the lower and upper bounds of
\|\boldsymbol{G}_{k}^{*}\|_{{\mathrm{F}}}
will affect the error bounds.

While the proposed model is linear in
\mathscr{A}
, the loss function in (
3.2
) is nonconvex with respect to
\boldsymbol{\omega}
and
\mathscr{G}
jointly. As an intermediate step to prove the consistency of the proposed estimators, the following lemma allows us to linearize
\mathscr{A}
with respect to
\boldsymbol{\omega}
and
\mathscr{G}
within a constant-radius neighborhood of
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{*}
; see Remark
3
for more details about the radius
c_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}
.

Lemma 1
.

Under Assumption
3
, for any
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}\times_{3}\boldsymbol{L}(\boldsymbol{\omega})
with
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N\times d}
and
\boldsymbol{\omega}\in\boldsymbol{\Omega}
, if
\|\boldsymbol{\omega}-\boldsymbol{\omega}^{*}\|_{2}\leq c_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}
, then

\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}\|_{{\mathrm{F}}}\asymp\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}^{*}\|_{{\mathrm{F}}}+\alpha\|\boldsymbol{\omega}-\boldsymbol{\omega}^{*}\|_{2}
,
where
c_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}>0
is a non-shrinking radius.

Note that any stationary VAR(
\infty
) process admits the VMA(
\infty
) representation,
\boldsymbol{y}_{t}=\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{*}(B)\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{t}
, where
B
is the backshift operator, and
\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{*}(B)=\boldsymbol{I}_{N}+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{j}^{*}B^{j}
; see Theorem
1
for a sufficient condition for the stationarity of the SARMA model. Here we suppress the dependence of
\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{j}^{*}
’s on
\boldsymbol{A}^{*}_{j}
’s and hence
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{*}
and
\boldsymbol{G}_{k}^{*}
’s for brevity. Let
\mu_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{*})=\min_{|z|=1}\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{*}(z)\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{*}^{{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{H}}}}(z))
and
\max_{|z|=1}\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{*}(z)\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{*}^{{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{H}}}}(z))
,
where
\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{*}^{{\mathrm{\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{H}}}}(z)
is the conjugate transpose of
\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{*}(z)
for
z\in\mathbb{C}
, and it can be verified that
\mu_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{*})>0
; see also
Basu and Michailidis, (2015)
. Then let

\kappa_{1}=\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon})\mu_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{*})\min\{1,c_{\bar{\rho}}^{2}\}
and

\kappa_{2}=\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon})\mu_{\max}(\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{*})\max\{1,C_{\bar{\rho}}^{2}\}
,
where
c_{\bar{\rho}},C_{\bar{\rho}}>0
are absolute constants defined in Lemma
S.2
in the supplementary file.

Theorem 3

(Rank-constrained estimator)
.

Let
d_{\pazocal{R}}=\pazocal{R}_{1}\pazocal{R}_{2}d+(\pazocal{R}_{1}+\pazocal{R}_{2})N
. Suppose that
\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{\omega}}-\boldsymbol{\omega}^{*}\|_{2}\leq c_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}
and

T\gtrsim(\kappa_{2}/\kappa_{1})^{2}d_{\pazocal{R}}\log(\kappa_{2}/\kappa_{1})
. Then under Assumptions
2
and
3
, with probability at least
1-4e^{-cd_{\pazocal{R}}\log(\kappa_{2}/\kappa_{1})}-8e^{-cN}-\{2+\sqrt{\kappa_{2}/\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon})}\}\sqrt{N/\{(\pazocal{R}_{1}+\pazocal{R}_{2})T\}}
, we have the following estimation and prediction error bounds:

\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}\|_{{\mathrm{F}}}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\kappa_{2}\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon})d_{\pazocal{R}}}{\kappa_{1}^{2}T}}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\|(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{A}}$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*})_{(1)}\boldsymbol{\widetilde{x}}_{t}\right\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim\frac{\kappa_{2}\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon})d_{\pazocal{R}}}{\kappa_{1}T}.

Combining Theorem
3
with Lemma
1
, we immediately have that with the same probability,
\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widehat{G}}$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}^{*}\|_{{\mathrm{F}}}\lesssim\sqrt{\kappa_{2}\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon})d_{\pazocal{R}}/(\kappa_{1}^{2}T)}
and
\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{\omega}}-\boldsymbol{\omega}^{*}\|_{2}\lesssim\sqrt{\kappa_{2}\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon})d_{\pazocal{R}}/(\alpha^{2}\kappa_{1}^{2}T)}
.

For the SLTR estimator, we make the following additional assumptions.

Assumption 4

(Sparsity)
.

Each column of the matrix
\boldsymbol{U}^{*}_{i}
has at most
s_{i}
nonzero entries, where
i=1,2
.

Assumption 5

(Restricted parameter space)
.

The parameter spaces for
\mathscr{S}
and
\boldsymbol{U}_{i}
with
i=1
or
2
are
\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\mbox{\boldmath\tiny$\mathscr{S}$}}=\{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{S}$}\in\mathrm{AO}(\pazocal{R}_{1},\pazocal{R}_{2}):\sigma_{1}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{S}$}_{(i)})\leq C_{\mbox{\boldmath\tiny$\mathscr{S}$}}<\infty,i=1,2\}
and
\pazocal{U}_{i}=\{\boldsymbol{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times\pazocal{R}_{i}}\mid\boldsymbol{U}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{U}=\boldsymbol{I}_{\pazocal{R}_{i}},\text{and }\boldsymbol{U}_{j,m}^{2}\geq\underline{u}>0\text{ or }\boldsymbol{U}_{j,m}^{2}=0,\forall 1\leq j\leq N,1\leq m\leq\pazocal{R}_{i}\}
, respectively, where
\underline{u}
is a uniform lower threshold, and
\boldsymbol{U}_{j,m}
is the
(j,m)
-th entry of the matrix
\boldsymbol{U}
.

Assumption 6

(Relative spectral gap)
.

The nonzero singular values of
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}_{(i)}
satisfy that
\sigma_{j-1}^{2}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}_{(i)})-\sigma_{j}^{2}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}_{(i)})\geq\beta\sigma_{j-1}^{2}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}_{(i)})
for
2\leq j\leq\pazocal{R}_{i}
and
i=1,2
, where
\beta>0
is a constant.

Assumption
4
defines the entrywise sparsity of
\boldsymbol{U}^{*}_{i}
’s. In Assumption
5
, the upper bound condition on
\mathscr{S}
is mild since large singular values in
\mathscr{S}
could cause nonstationarity of the process. The lower threshold
\underline{u}
for
\boldsymbol{U}_{i}
’s is needed to establish the restricted eigenvalue condition
(
Bickel et al.,, 2009
)
. Since
\underline{u}
may shrink to zero as the dimension increases, this is not a stringent condition. Assumption
6
requires that the singular values of
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}_{(i)}
’s are well separated to ensure identifiability. See
Wang et al., (2022)
for similar assumptions.
The consistency of the SLTR estimator is established as follows.

Theorem 4

(SLTR estimator)
.

Let
d_{\pazocal{S}}=\pazocal{R}_{1}\pazocal{R}_{2}d+\sum_{i=1}^{2}s_{i}\pazocal{R}_{i}\log(N\pazocal{R}_{i})
. Suppose that
\|\boldsymbol{\widetilde{\omega}}-\boldsymbol{\omega}^{*}\|_{2}\leq c_{\omega}
and
T\gtrsim d_{\pazocal{S}}+\underline{u}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{2}\pazocal{R}_{i}\log(N\pazocal{R}_{i})+\underline{u}^{-2}(s_{1}\vee s_{2})(\pazocal{R}_{1}\vee\pazocal{R}_{2})(d+\log N)
. Then under Assumptions
2
–
6
, if
\lambda\gtrsim\sqrt{\kappa_{2}\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon})d_{\pazocal{S}}/{T}}
, with probability at least
1-5e^{-cd_{\pazocal{S}}-c\underline{u}^{-1}(\pazocal{R}_{1}+\pazocal{R}_{2}+\log N\pazocal{R}_{1}+\log N\pazocal{R}_{2})}-7e^{-cs_{2}\log N(\pazocal{R}_{1}\wedge\pazocal{R}_{2})}-c\sqrt{(s_{2}+\underline{u}^{-1})\pazocal{R}_{2}/T}(1+\sqrt{(s_{2}+\underline{u}^{-1})\pazocal{R}_{2}/d_{\pazocal{S}}})
, it holds

\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widetilde{A}}$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}\|_{{\mathrm{F}}}\lesssim\frac{(\eta_{1}+\eta_{2})\sqrt{s_{1}+s_{2}}\lambda}{\beta\kappa_{1}}\hskip 5.69054pt\text{and}\hskip 5.69054pt\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widetilde{A}}$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*})_{(1)}\boldsymbol{\widetilde{x}}_{t}\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim\frac{(\eta_{1}+\eta_{2})^{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})\lambda^{2}}{\beta^{2}\kappa_{1}},

where
\eta_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{\pazocal{R}_{i}}\sigma_{1}^{2}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}_{(i)}^{*})/\sigma_{j}^{2}(\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}_{(i)}^{*})
for
i=1,2
.

Taking
\lambda\asymp\sqrt{\kappa_{2}\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon})d_{\pazocal{S}}/{T}}
, the estimation and prediction error bounds in Theorem
4
become
(\eta_{1}+\eta_{2})\sqrt{(s_{1}+s_{2})\kappa_{2}\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon})d_{\pazocal{S}}/(\beta^{2}\kappa_{1}^{2}T)}
and
(\eta_{1}+\eta_{2})(s_{1}+s_{2})\kappa_{2}\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon})d_{\pazocal{S}}/(\beta^{2}\kappa_{1}T)
, respectively. Then, in view of Lemma
1
, the high probability bounds for
\|\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{\widetilde{G}}$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}^{*}\|_{{\mathrm{F}}}
and
\|\boldsymbol{\widetilde{\omega}}-\boldsymbol{\omega}^{*}\|_{2}
can be easily obtained.

In practice, the ranks
\pazocal{R}_{1},\pazocal{R}_{2}
and model orders
p,r,s
are usually small. Then by fixing the constants
\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon})
,
\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\varepsilon})
,
\mu_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{*})
,
\mu_{\max}(\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{*})
,
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}
and
\beta
, the estimation error bound for the rank-constrained estimator
\mathscr{\widehat{A}}
can be simplified to
\sqrt{N/T}
, while that for the SLTR estimator
\mathscr{\widetilde{A}}
reduces to
\sqrt{(s_{1}+s_{2})^{2}\log(N)/T}
.

Remark 3
.

We give more details about the non-shrinking radius
c_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}
in Lemma
1
. The result of Lemma
1
comes from the following first-order Taylor expansion:

\boldsymbol{\Delta}(\boldsymbol{\omega},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$})=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{{A}}$}(\boldsymbol{\omega},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$})-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{A}$}^{*}=\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{M}$}(\boldsymbol{\omega}-\boldsymbol{\omega}^{*},\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}-\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{G}$}^{*})\times_{3}\boldsymbol{L}_{\rm{stack}}(\boldsymbol{\omega}^{*})+\text{remainder}
,
where
\mbox{\boldmath$\mathscr{M}$}:\mathbb{R}^{r+2s}\times\mathbb{R}^{N\times N\times d}\to\mathbb{R}^{N\times N\times(d+r+2s)}
is a bilinear function, and
\boldsymbol{L}_{\rm{stack}}(\boldsymbol{\omega}^{*})
is a
\infty\times(d+r+2s)
constant matrix; see the proof of Lemma
1
in the supplementary file. The negligibility of the remainder term requires that
\boldsymbol{\omega}
lies within a constant radius of
\boldsymbol{\omega}^{*}
. In our proof, we derive the radius

c_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}=\min\left\{2,\frac{c_{\mbox{\boldmath\tiny$\mathscr{G}$}}(1-\bar{\rho})\sigma_{\min,L}}{8\sqrt{2}C_{L}}\right\}
,
where
\sigma_{\min,L}:=\sigma_{\min}(\boldsymbol{L}_{\rm{stack}}(\boldsymbol{\omega}^{*}))
,
c_{\mbox{\boldmath\tiny$\mathscr{G}$}}:=\min_{p+1\leq k\leq d}\|\boldsymbol{G}_{k}^{*}\|_{{\mathrm{F}}}/\max_{p+1\leq k\leq d}\|\boldsymbol{G}_{k}^{*}\|_{{\mathrm{F}}}
, and
C_{L}>0
is an absolute constant given in Lemma
S.1
in the supplementary file. Note that Assumption
3
implies that
\sigma_{\min,L}>0
and
c_{\mbox{\boldmath\tiny$\mathscr{G}$}}>0
are both absolute constants: the former is shown by Lemma
S.2
in the supplementary file, and the latter is a direct consequence of Assumption
3
(iii). Thus, the radius
c_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}
is non-shrinking.

Remark 4
.

In the proofs of Theorems
3
and
4
, we show that the effect of initial values for
\{\boldsymbol{y}_{t},t\leq 0\}
has no contribution to the final estimation error rates; see the quantities
|S_{i}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Delta}})|
for
1\leq i\leq 3
in the supplementary file. We bound the initialization error terms by Markov’s inequality, resulting in a nonexponential tail probability, which may be sharpened by employing more sophisticated concentration inequalities.

Remark 5
.

The ranks
\pazocal{R}_{1},\pazocal{R}_{2}
and model orders
p,r,s
are unknown in practice, but can be selected by data-driven methods. Due the page limit, we discuss them in Section
S1
of the supplementary material. We present an easy-to-implement estimator of the ranks, which is proved to be consistent. Model order selection via the Bayesian information criterion is also discussed therein.

4
Two empirical examples

4.1
Macroeconomic dataset

This dataset contains observations of 20 quarterly macroeconomic variables from June 1959 to December 2019, with
T=243
, retrieved from FRED-QD
(
McCracken and Ng,, 2016
)
. These variables come from four categories: (i) stock market, (ii) exchange rates, (iii) money and credit, and (iv) interest rates. These categories are usually considered in the construction of financial condition index, since they reflect important factors that can affect the stance of monetary policy and aggregate demand conditions

(
Goodhart et al.,, 2001
;
Bulut,, 2016
;
Hatzius et al.,, 2010
)
. All series are transformed to be stationary, and standardized to have zero mean and unit variance; see the supplementary file for more details of the variables and their transformations.

We first explore the factor structures of this dataset. As discussed in Section
2.4
,
\boldsymbol{U}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{U}_{2}
capture response factor and predictor factor spaces, respectively. By the rank selection method in Section
S1
, we obtain
(\widehat{\pazocal{R}}_{1},\widehat{\pazocal{R}}_{2})=(3,3)
.
Figure
1
displays
\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}_{1}
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}_{2}
based on the proposed SLTR estimation in Section
2.4
, where the regularization parameter is selected by cross-validation. Overall, it can be observed that the response factors (RFs) are mainly influenced by variables in categories (i) and (iv) and business loan indicator from category (iii), while the influence from categories (ii) is relatively weak. On the other hand, only the S&P 500 index from category (i) and category (iv) contributes significantly to the predictor factors (PFs).

Figure 1
:
SLTR estimates of factor loadings in the proposed SARMA model for the macroeconomic and realized volatility datasets. Response factors (“RF”s) correspond to columns of
\boldsymbol{U}_{1}
while predictor factors (“PF”s) correspond to columns of
\boldsymbol{U}_{2}
.

We evaluate the performance of our method based on out-of-sample forecast accuracy. The following rolling forecast procedure is adopted: we first fit the models using historical data with the end point rolling from the fourth quarter of 2015 to the third quarter of 2019, and then conduct one-step-ahead forecasts based on the fitted models. In addition to the proposed rank-constrained (RC) and SLTR estimators, we consider five other existing methods, including three based on the VAR model and two based on the VARMA model. Specifically, for the VAR model, we consider (a) the Lasso method
(
Basu and Michailidis,, 2015
)
and two methods in
Wang et al., (2022)
: (b) the multilinear low-rank (MLR) method and (c) the sparse higher-order reduced-rank (SHORR) method, which further imposes sparsity on the factor matrices in (b) using a slightly different regularizer than the method in this paper.
For the VARMA model, we apply the method in
Wilms et al., (2023)
with (d) the
\ell_{1}
-penalty or (e) the HLag penalty. Note that (a) is used as the Phase-I estimator for the estimators in (d) and (e), and the AR order is selected according to
Wilms et al., (2023)
. The AR order for (b) and (c) is chosen as in
Wang et al., (2022)
. For the proposed low-Tucker-rank SARMA model, the estimated model orders are
(\widehat{p},\widehat{r},\widehat{s})=(0,1,0)
.
Throughout the rolling forecast procedure, the same model orders and ranks are used.

Table
1
reports the mean squared forecast error (MSFE) and mean absolute forecast error (MAFE) for all methods. It can be observed that the proposed methods achieve the smallest forecast errors among all competing ones. Compared to sparse but non-low-rank models, i.e., (a), (d) and (e), the proposed model can better capture the factor structure which is prominent in this dataset. Meanwhile, its higher flexibility than the VAR model is supported by its better forecasting performance than (b) and (c). In addition, note that imposing sparsity on the factor matrices generally results in smaller forecast errors for both VAR and SARMA models; see Figure
1
.
